# ROUND 2/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'll analyze the aggregation mission and design finite-state machines to minimize the score.

## Reasoning

The mission: robots start scattered in the center (GRAY floor). The target region has BLUE bordering walls. Score increases for every robot outside the target region. So robots need to:
1. Navigate toward the BLUE-walled strip (which has BLACK floor)
2. Stay there

Key perceptions:
- Floor color: BLACK = target strip or other strip; GRAY = middle area
- Wall colors: BLUE = target side, GREEN = other side
- Camera can detect colored objects (walls)

Strategy:
- **GoToColor toward BLUE (clr=2)**: move toward blue walls to reach target strip
- **GoAwayColor from GREEN (clr=3)**: avoid the green-walled strip  
- **Floor BLACK condition**: detect when in a black strip
- **Camera sees BLUE**: confirms target side

Wait - the score is minimized when robots are IN the target region (BLACK floor, BLUE walls). The other strip also has BLACK floor but GREEN walls. So robots need to distinguish: go toward BLUE walls, avoid GREEN walls.

**Design approach:**
- State: GoToColor BLUE → moves toward blue walls
- When on BLACK floor AND sees BLUE → Stop (stay in target)
- When on BLACK floor but sees GREEN → GoAwayColor GREEN or turn around

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I need to see the results from Round 2 to know which machines performed best. Since no scores were provided, I'll reason from first principles and diversify my designs.

## Reasoning

The mission requires robots to reach the BLUE-walled strip (BLACK floor). Key insight:
- Both strips have BLACK floor; center is GRAY
- BLUE walls = target; GREEN walls = wrong strip
- Robots need to: detect BLUE walls → move toward them → stay when on BLACK floor near BLUE

The core strategy: **GoToColor BLUE** to approach target, **Stop** when on BLACK floor (with protection against GREEN side), and **GoAwayColor GREEN** as a correction behavior.

I'll include the elitism copies of best-topology machines plus variations and new explorations. Since I don't have explicit scores, I'll keep all 14 machines from Round 2 slightly varied plus new topologies.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I notice I'm not receiving score feedback between rounds. I'll reason more carefully about the problem and diversify more aggressively.

## Reasoning

**Re-examining the problem:**
- Score = sum over time of robots outside target region
- Target = strip with BLUE walls, BLACK floor
- Other strip = GREEN walls, BLACK floor  
- Center = GRAY floor
- Robots start in center (GRAY)

**Critical insight I may have missed:** The color IDs for walls:
- BLUE = color 4 (not 2!) in ARGoS. Let me reconsider: clr values {1=red, 2=green(?), 3=blue(?), 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan}. Actually the color mapping isn't specified explicitly. BLUE walls likely map to clr=3 or similar.

Actually re-reading: `--clr<I> one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}` - these are just indices. The problem says BLUE walls = target. I'll try multiple color indices to hedge bets.

**Best strategy:** GoToColor toward blue walls (try different clr values), stop on black floor, escape if seeing green.

I'll include machines targeting different color indices to cover uncertainty, plus pure exploration/repulsion approaches.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still haven't received score feedback. Let me reconsider the problem more carefully.

## Reasoning

**Fundamental re-analysis:**

The mission says "AGGREGATION" but the scoring is about robots being in the target region (BLUE-walled strip, BLACK floor). This is actually a **go-to-region** task, not a clustering task.

**Key perception issue:** I've been uncertain about which color index maps to BLUE/GREEN walls. Let me think about ARGoS color conventions: typically 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. So BLUE walls = clr=3, GREEN walls = clr=2.

**Simple winning strategy:** 
- GoToColor BLUE (clr=3): move toward blue walls
- Stop when on BLACK floor (you've arrived)
- If you see GREEN walls while on BLACK floor, you're in the wrong strip → move away

**Why random walk + stop-on-black might work:** Robots wander until they hit a black strip, then stop. But they might stop in the wrong (GREEN) strip.

The key discriminator: once on BLACK floor, check if you see BLUE or GREEN walls. If GREEN → keep moving.

I'm still not receiving score feedback. Let me fundamentally rethink my approach.

## Reasoning

**Critical re-examination:**

I've been getting no feedback on scores, which means I need to think more carefully. Let me reconsider the color mapping and task structure.

The problem says "AGGREGATION mission" but scoring is about being in the target region. The title may just be the mission name in the AutoMoDe framework.

**Color mapping in ARGoS/AutoMoDe:** Based on AutoMoDe documentation: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. So BLUE walls = clr=3, GREEN walls = clr=2. This seems right.

**The simplest possible approaches that should work:**

1. **Pure random walk + stop on black**: Robots wander until they hit black floor. Problem: they might stop in the wrong strip.

2. **GoToColor blue + stop on black**: Should work if blue walls are visible from center. The robots start in the center - can they see the walls? With camera range, likely yes.

3. **The escape problem**: If robot lands in green strip (black floor, green walls), it needs to leave. Need: if on black AND see green → move away from green OR toward blue.

**Key fix**: State 1 (stopped on black) should transition back to GoToColor-blue if it sees green walls.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'm still not receiving score data. Let me take a completely fresh approach and think more carefully about what might actually work.

## Reasoning

**Fundamental rethink:**

I've been assuming robots can see colored walls with their camera. But the camera condition (C=7) detects colored *objects* - in ARGoS, walls may or may not be colored objects visible to the camera. 

**What robots CAN definitely perceive:**
- Floor color under them (BLACK/GRAY/WHITE) - conditions C=0, C=1, C=2
- Number of neighbors (C=3, C=4)
- Fixed probability (C=5)

**The floor color approach:**
- GRAY = center (bad, need to leave)
- BLACK = one of the two strips (could be good or bad)

**Problem:** Can't distinguish the two black strips by floor color alone. But robots starting in center need to reach blue strip. The blue strip is at ONE end of the arena.

**Key insight I've been missing:** Maybe the simplest approach - just explore until you hit black, then stop - gets roughly half the robots in the right place. But we need better.

**Alternative:** Use neighbor-count signals. If robots aggregate in the target (encouraged by attraction), they reinforce each other there. But the score doesn't reward clustering per se.

**Actually:** Let me try pure random walk stopping on black - it's simple and gets ~50% right. Then add GoAwayColor green to escape wrong strip. Also try attraction to neighbors already in black strips.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still have no score feedback. Let me think very differently about what's actually happening.

## Reasoning

**Complete rethink of the problem:**

The task is called "AGGREGATION" in AutoMoDe. The scoring penalizes robots outside the target region. But wait - maybe I'm misreading the task. Let me re-read: "score increases by 1 for every robot outside the target region." Target = BLUE-walled strip, BLACK floor.

**What if the camera simply doesn't work well for wall detection in this setup?** The condition C=7 detects colored objects. If walls aren't colored objects but just boundaries, this won't work.

**What if I focus purely on floor color?**
- Start in GRAY (center)
- Move until BLACK floor detected → stop
- But can't distinguish which BLACK strip

**The key insight I keep missing:** Both strips are BLACK. Without wall color detection, robots can't tell which strip they're in. So the best floor-only strategy gives ~50% correct placement.

**What if I use a directional bias?** GoToColor might track colored walls IF they're implemented as colored objects. But I've been uncertain about this.

**New approach - try ALL color indices systematically:** Colors 1-6, trying to find which one corresponds to blue walls in this specific ARGoS setup.

Let me also try: exploration with neighbor attraction to exploit any clustering that emerges, and spiral movements.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
Still no score feedback. Let me reason very carefully about what could actually work.

## Reasoning

**Deep re-examination of the problem:**

The scoring: 1 point per robot per step outside target. 20 robots × 1200 steps = 24000 max score. A perfect solution scores 0.

**What I know for certain:**
- Floor BLACK = robot is in one of the two strips (target or wrong)
- Floor GRAY = robot is in the center (bad)
- The two strips are indistinguishable by floor color alone

**The camera (C=7):** Detects colored objects. In AutoMoDe/ARGoS, walls ARE colored objects detectable by the omnidirectional camera. BLUE walls (target side) vs GREEN walls (wrong side). Color indices: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan.

So BLUE walls = clr=3, GREEN walls = clr=2. This is what I've been trying.

**Why might my machines be failing?**
- Maybe GoToColor doesn't work well when the target color is walls (too large, fills whole view)
- Maybe the transition probability needs tuning
- Maybe the escape from wrong strip is the critical piece

**New hypothesis:** The simplest working design is: Explore → stop on black → if you see green walls, go back to exploring. The stop-on-black traps robots in whichever strip they reach first. With random walks, ~50% end up in each strip. The score would be ~50% of max = ~12000. We need better.

**The real fix:** When stopped on black AND seeing green walls → actively move toward blue walls (GoToColor clr=3). This should work if camera detects walls.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
This is the final round. Still no explicit score feedback, but I need to make my best possible guesses.

## Reasoning

**Final analysis:**

After 9 rounds of trying various approaches without feedback, I need to cover the most promising strategies comprehensively:

1. **Single-state GoToColor** (clr=1,2,3,4,5,6) - pure directional approach, always moving toward colored walls. This is the simplest and if the right color index matches BLUE, it should work perfectly - robots just keep pressing against the blue wall.

2. **GoToColor + Stop on Black** - move toward blue wall, stop when floor is black (arrived in strip). Risk: wrong strip. Add escape via green-wall detection.

3. **Random walk + Stop on Black** - simple baseline that gets ~50% right.

4. **The key insight for final round:** A single-state FSM that just does `GoToColor clr=X` with no transitions will continuously push robots toward whatever wall is that color. If clr=3 = blue, ALL robots end up at the blue wall = score near 0. This is the theoretically optimal behavior if it works.

I'll saturate coverage of all 6 color indices for single-state GoToColor, plus best multi-state combinations.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
